# 16. OpenCV Core Architecture - Matrix Representation, Channels, and Color Constellations

## 1. Mathematical and Computational Overview
Digital computer vision algorithms operate on digitized spatial energy fields. OpenCV (Open Source Computer Vision Library) models an image as a discretized, bounded two-dimensional or three-dimensional tensor mapped onto standard NumPy `ndarray` memory buffers.

```mermaid
mindmap
  root((OpenCV Architecture))
    Memory Representation
      NumPy Array Interface
      Contiguous C-Buffers
      Row-Major Memory Order
    Spatial Dimensions
      Height Rows Y-Axis
      Width Columns X-Axis
      Channel Depth Z-Axis
    Color Constellations
      Native BGR Format
      Grayscale Luminance Y
      Additive RGB
      CIE L*a*b* Perceptual
```

## 2. Theoretical Foundations: Tensor Topologies

A digital image $I$ is mathematically represented as a discrete 2D matrix or 3D tensor defined on a spatial coordinate domain:

$$\Omega = \{ (y, x) \in \mathbb{Z}^2 \mid 0 \le y < H, \ 0 \le x < W \}$$

Where:
- $H$ represents image height (total rows).
- $W$ represents image width (total columns).
- $y \in [0, H-1]$ denotes the vertical scanline index.
- $x \in [0, W-1]$ denotes the horizontal column index.

### 2.1 Single-Channel Grayscale Matrices
For monochrome intensity imaging, each coordinate pair $(y, x)$ maps directly to a scalar radiance value $I(y, x) \in [0, 255]$, stored as an unsigned 8-bit integer (`numpy.uint8`):

$$I : \Omega \rightarrow [0, 2^{8}-1]$$

### 2.2 Multi-Channel Color Arrays and the BGR Convention
Color digital photography represents chromatic information via multiple discrete spectral bands. While standard display graphics operate in the red-green-blue (RGB) format, OpenCV historically adopted **BGR (Blue, Green, Red)** order due to early hardware and video capture standards:

- **Channel 0**: Blue photon absorption intensity ($\lambda \approx 450\text{ nm}$).
- **Channel 1**: Green photon absorption intensity ($\lambda \approx 540\text{ nm}$).
- **Channel 2**: Red photon absorption intensity ($\lambda \approx 700\text{ nm}$).

The memory layout of a pixel located at scanline $y$ and column $x$ is arranged consecutively in RAM as:

$$\text{Pixel}(y, x) = [B_{(y, x)}, \ G_{(y, x)}, \ R_{(y, x)}]$$

## 3. Systematic Function Reference

### 3.1 `cv2.imread(filename, flags)`
Loads an image matrix from non-volatile storage into volatile system RAM.
- **Parameters**:
  - `filename` (`str`): File path to the target image raster (e.g., `.bmp`, `.jpg`, `.png`).
  - `flags` (`int`): Ingestion mode flag.
    - `cv2.IMREAD_COLOR` ($1$): Forces ingestion as a 3-channel BGR image. Discards alpha transparency.
    - `cv2.IMREAD_GRAYSCALE` ($0$): Forces single-channel luminance reduction during decoding.
    - `cv2.IMREAD_UNCHANGED` ($-1$): Preserves native bit-depth and transparency channels.
- **Return Value**: An instantiated `numpy.ndarray`. Returns `None` if the file path is invalid or the codec fails.

### 3.2 `cv2.cvtColor(src, code)`
Executes linear or non-linear radiometric space transformations across image planes.
- **Mathematical Transformation (`cv2.COLOR_BGR2GRAY`)**:
  Calculates human photopic luminance ($Y$) according to ITU-R Recommendation BT.601:
  $$Y = 0.299 \cdot R + 0.587 \cdot G + 0.114 \cdot B$$
  The green channel receives the highest weighting ($\approx 59\%$) because the human ocular system exhibits maximum cone sensitivity near $555\text{ nm}$.
- **Mathematical Transformation (`cv2.COLOR_BGR2RGB`)**:
  Swaps outer channels:
  $$[R, G, B] = [B_{\text{old}}, G_{\text{old}}, R_{\text{old}}][::-1]$$

### 3.3 `cv2.imshow(winname, mat)` and Display Loop Mechanics
- `cv2.imshow(winname, mat)`: Renders pixel values within an operating system GUI window.
- `cv2.waitKey(delay)`: HighGUI event listener. Suspends CPU execution for `delay` milliseconds, checking the OS event queue for keyboard input. Setting `delay = 0` halts execution indefinitely until a keypress occurs.
- `cv2.destroyAllWindows()`: Deallocates all initialized X11, Win32, or Cocoa window handles from memory.

### 3.4 `cv2.imwrite(filename, img)`
Serializes in-memory NumPy tensors into standard image file formats, handling compression codecs automatically.

## 4. Code Implementation Examples

```python
import cv2
import numpy as np

# Ingest test image
bgr_image = cv2.imread("Images/BoatsColor.bmp", cv2.IMREAD_COLOR)

if bgr_image is None:
    raise FileNotFoundError("Image file 'Images/BoatsColor.bmp' could not be opened.")

# Inspect structural tensor properties
height, width, channels = bgr_image.shape
data_type = bgr_image.dtype
total_bytes = bgr_image.nbytes

print(f"Matrix Geometry: Height={height} rows, Width={width} columns, Depth={channels} channels")
print(f"Memory Buffer : Data type={data_type}, Total RAM footprint={total_bytes:,} bytes")

# Read color vector at coordinate y=100, x=150
b_val, g_val, r_val = bgr_image[100, 150]
print(f"Pixel Spectral Signature at (y=100, x=150): Blue={b_val}, Green={g_val}, Red={r_val}")

# Direct scanline manipulation: Draw horizontal pure red line across the middle
# Pure Red in BGR format is [0, 0, 255]
annotated_image = bgr_image.copy()
center_row = height // 2

# Vectorized scanline overwrite across all columns
annotated_image[center_row, :] = [0, 0, 255]

# Convert BGR to Grayscale using BT.601 weights
gray_image = cv2.cvtColor(bgr_image, cv2.COLOR_BGR2GRAY)

# Save results to disk
cv2.imwrite("Images/BoatsColor_red_centerline.bmp", annotated_image)
cv2.imwrite("Images/BoatsColor_grayscale_converted.bmp", gray_image)
```

## 5. Critical Pitfalls and Best Practices
- **OpenCV vs. Matplotlib Color Inversion**: Matplotlib expects RGB pixel arrays. Passing a raw OpenCV BGR image directly into `plt.imshow()` causes color channels to flip: human faces appear blue and skies appear orange. Always apply `cv2.cvtColor(img, cv2.COLOR_BGR2RGB)` before visualizing with Matplotlib.
- **Coordinate Order Inversion ($y, x$ vs. $x, y$)**: In NumPy indexing, row index corresponds to $y$ and column index corresponds to $x$ (`img[y, x]`). In OpenCV geometry functions (like `cv2.circle` or `cv2.rectangle`), coordinate tuples use Cartesian order: `(x, y)`. Mixing up these conventions can lead to out-of-bounds indexing errors.

## 6. Connections to Other Topics
- Relates directly to array memory layouts in [[01. NumPy Foundations - Memory Layout, ndarray Anatomy, and Array Instantiation]].
- Forms the basis for slicing in [[17. Image Spatial Transformations - Interpolation, Scaling, Cropping, and Affine Warping]].
- Applied practically in [[06.1. Digital Image Acquisition, Matrix Topologies, and Fixed Thresholding (TP1)]].
```

## Integrated Subject Knowledge: 01.11. OpenCV Color Space Transformations and Pixel Math

OpenCV provides functions for converting images between color coordinate spaces and performing bounded saturation arithmetic.

```mermaid
mindmap
  root((Color Math and Spaces))
    Color Conversion
      cv2.cvtColor Engine
      COLOR_BGR2GRAY
      COLOR_BGR2HSV
      COLOR_BGR2LAB
    Saturating Pixel Arithmetic
      cv2.add Clamped Addition
      cv2.subtract Clamped Subtraction
      Comparison to Modulo NumPy
```

### 1. Color Space Transformations: `cv2.cvtColor(src, code)`
Converts an input image from one color space to another.

* `src`: The input NumPy array.
* `code`: The transformation flag specifying the source and target color spaces.
  * `cv2.COLOR_BGR2GRAY`: Computes luminance using the standard photometric formula: $Y = 0.299R + 0.587G + 0.114B$.
  * `cv2.COLOR_BGR2HSV`: Maps color values to Hue, Saturation, and Value components.
  * `cv2.COLOR_BGR2LAB`: Maps colors to the perceptually uniform CIE $L^*a^*b^*$ space.

```python
import cv2

bgr_input = cv2.imread('scene.png', cv2.IMREAD_COLOR)

# Transform to single-channel luminance
gray = cv2.cvtColor(bgr_input, cv2.COLOR_BGR2GRAY)

# Transform to CIE L*a*b* space for contrast enhancement
lab = cv2.cvtColor(bgr_input, cv2.COLOR_BGR2LAB)
```

### 2. Saturated vs. Modulo Arithmetic
A critical difference between OpenCV and NumPy is how they handle arithmetic overflow:
* **NumPy** uses standard computer arithmetic with modulo wrap-around:
  $$250_{\text{uint8}} + 20_{\text{uint8}} = 270 \pmod{256} = 14$$
* **OpenCV** uses saturated arithmetic that clamps values to the dynamic range:
  $$\text{cv2.add}(250_{\text{uint8}}, 20_{\text{uint8}}) = \min(270, 255) = 255$$
  $$\text{cv2.subtract}(10_{\text{uint8}}, 30_{\text{uint8}}) = \max(-20, 0) = 0$$

```python
import numpy as np
import cv2

p1 = np.array([250], dtype=np.uint8)
p2 = np.array([20], dtype=np.uint8)

print("NumPy Modulo Wrap-Around:", p1 + p2)             # Yields [14]
print("OpenCV Saturated Clamping:", cv2.add(p1, p2))    # Yields [[255]]
```

Related concepts: [[01.10. OpenCV Fundamentals - Image Ingestion, Channels, and Memory Buffers]], [[02.6. Perceptual and Broadcast Spaces - YUV, YCbCr, HSV, and Perceptually Uniform Lab]], [[03.14. Tonal Modification - Dynamic Range Expansion, Global HE, AHE, and CLAHE]].

### 01.12. OpenCV Geometric Transformations, Interpolation, and Affine Warping.md

# OpenCV Geometric Transformations, Interpolation, and Affine Warping

Geometric transformations alter the spatial layout of pixels by mapping input coordinates $(x, y)$ to new output coordinates $(x', y')$.

```mermaid
mindmap
  root((Geometric Transformations))
    Spatial Resizing
      cv2.resize Primitive
      Interpolation Flags
    Affine Warping
      cv2.warpAffine Transform
      cv2.getRotationMatrix2D
      Forward vs Inverse Mapping
```

### 1. Spatial Resizing: `cv2.resize(src, dsize, fx=0, fy=0, interpolation=cv2.INTER_LINEAR)`
Rescales an image to a new spatial resolution.

* `dsize`: The output image dimensions given as a tuple of **`(width, height)`**. **Note:** This is the reverse of NumPy's `(rows, cols)` shape order!
* `fx`, `fy`: Optional scale factors along the horizontal and vertical axes when `dsize` is set to `(0, 0)`.
* `interpolation`: The analytical reconstruction algorithm:
  * `cv2.INTER_NEAREST`: Nearest-neighbor sampling. Fast, but produces visible pixelation and jagged edges.
  * `cv2.INTER_LINEAR`: Bilinear interpolation. Balanced performance; standard for general upsampling.
  * `cv2.INTER_CUBIC`: Bicubic spline interpolation over a $4 \times 4$ neighborhood. Produces sharper edges with fewer artifacts.
  * `cv2.INTER_AREA`: Resampling using pixel area relations. Preferred for downsampling images without aliasing.

```python
import cv2

img = cv2.imread('sample.png', cv2.IMREAD_GRAYSCALE)
h, w = img.shape[:2]

# Scale image by factor of 2 using Bicubic interpolation
upscaled = cv2.resize(img, (w * 2, h * 2), interpolation=cv2.INTER_CUBIC)
```

### 2. Affine Transformation: `cv2.warpAffine(src, M, dsize)`
Applies an affine transformation defined by a $2 \times 3$ transformation matrix:

$$\begin{pmatrix} x' \\ y' \end{pmatrix} = \begin{pmatrix} m_{00} & m_{01} \\ m_{10} & m_{11} \end{pmatrix} \begin{pmatrix} x \\ y \end{pmatrix} + \begin{pmatrix} m_{02} \\ m_{12} \end{pmatrix}$$

#### Image Rotation: `cv2.getRotationMatrix2D(center, angle, scale)`
Constructs the affine matrix for rotating an image around a specified center point:

```python
# Rotate 45 degrees counter-clockwise around the image center
center_point = (w // 2, h // 2)
rotation_matrix = cv2.getRotationMatrix2D(center=center_point, angle=45.0, scale=1.0)

# Warp the image onto an output canvas of size (w, h)
rotated_img = cv2.warpAffine(img, rotation_matrix, (w, h))
```

Related concepts: [[05.1. Continuous Image Reconstruction - Nearest-Neighbor, Bilinear, and Keys' Bicubic Spline]], [[06.10. Worked Image Processing Exercises and End-to-End Solutions]].


========================================================================
 FILE: TAI APDS Vault/Libraries/17. Image Spatial Transformations - Interpolation, Scaling, Cropping, and Affine Warping.md
   Language: markdown
========================================================================

# 17. Image Spatial Transformations - Interpolation, Scaling, Cropping, and Affine Warping

## 1. Overview
Geometric and spatial transformations map pixel coordinates from an input image plane onto an output raster grid. This note covers coordinate mapping, spatial cropping via Region of Interest (ROI) slicing, geometric scaling, interpolation methods, and 2D matrix rotations.

```mermaid
mindmap
  root((Spatial Transformations))
    Coordinate Extraction
      NumPy Tensor Slicing
      Bounding Box ROIs
      Memory Views vs Deep Clones
    Resampling Operators
      Nearest Neighbor INTER_NEAREST
      Bilinear INTER_LINEAR
      Bicubic INTER_CUBIC
    Geometric Warping
      Orthogonal Rotation
      Affine Transformation Matrices
      Text and Vector Overlay
```

## 2. Theoretical Foundations: Coordinate Mapping and Resampling

A spatial transformation is a mathematical mapping $T$ that relates input coordinates $(x, y)$ to output target coordinates $(x', y')$:

$$\begin{bmatrix} x' \\ y' \end{bmatrix} = T \begin{bmatrix} x \\ y \end{bmatrix}$$

Because transformed coordinates $(x', y')$ often fall on non-integer sub-pixel locations, discrete intensity values must be reconstructed using **spatial interpolation**.

```mermaid
graph TD
    A[Output Pixel Grid Coordinate x', y'] --> B[Backward Mapping T^-1 to Input Plane]
    B --> C{Sub-Pixel Position Detection}
    C -->|Zero-Order Hold| D[Nearest Neighbor: Select Closest Discrete Node]
    C -->|First-Order Polynomial| E[Bilinear: Convex Combination of 4 Neighbors]
    C -->|Third-Order Cubic Spline| F[Bicubic: 16-Point Neighborhood Convolution]
```

### 2.1 Nearest Neighbor Interpolation (`cv2.INTER_NEAREST`)
Assigns the intensity of the nearest discrete coordinate:
$$I(x', y') = I(\text{round}(x), \ \text{round}(y))$$
- **Complexity**: $O(1)$ per target pixel.
- **Trade-off**: Extremely fast, but introduces jagged staircase artifacts along diagonal boundaries.

### 2.2 Bilinear Interpolation (`cv2.INTER_LINEAR`)
Performs linear interpolation sequentially across both axes over a $2 \times 2$ neighborhood. Let $(x_1, y_1)$, $(x_1, y_2)$, $(x_2, y_1)$, and $(x_2, y_2)$ denote the four neighboring pixel coordinates surrounding sub-pixel point $(x, y)$:

$$I(x, y_1) \approx \frac{x_2 - x}{x_2 - x_1} I(x_1, y_1) + \frac{x - x_1}{x_2 - x_1} I(x_2, y_1)$$

$$I(x, y_2) \approx \frac{x_2 - x}{x_2 - x_1} I(x_1, y_2) + \frac{x - x_1}{x_2 - x_1} I(x_2, y_2)$$

$$I(x, y) \approx \frac{y_2 - y}{y_2 - y_1} I(x, y_1) + \frac{y - y_1}{y_2 - y_1} I(x, y_2)$$

### 2.3 Bicubic Interpolation (`cv2.INTER_CUBIC`)
Interpolates across a $4 \times 4$ neighborhood (16 pixels) using cubic convolution kernels. It balances sharpness and smooth gradients while reducing pixelation artifacts.

## 3. Core Functions Reference

### 3.1 Region of Interest (ROI) Slicing
Because OpenCV images are stored as NumPy arrays, spatial sub-regions are extracted using standard slicing syntax:
$$\text{ROI} = I[y_{\text{start}}:y_{\text{end}}, \ x_{\text{start}}:x_{\text{end}}]$$
*Crucial Architecture Note*: Standard slicing returns a **memory view**. Modifying values inside an uncopied ROI alters the source image directly. Use `.copy()` to ensure data isolation.

### 3.2 `cv2.resize(src, dsize, fx, fy, interpolation)`
Rescales image raster dimensions.
- `src`: Input source tensor.
- `dsize`: Target output resolution specified as a tuple: `(width, height)`.
- `fx, fy`: Scale factor multipliers along the horizontal and vertical axes when `dsize = (0, 0)`.
- `interpolation`: Mathematical sampling algorithm (`cv2.INTER_NEAREST`, `cv2.INTER_LINEAR`, `cv2.INTER_CUBIC`, `cv2.INTER_AREA`).

### 3.3 `cv2.rotate(src, rotateCode)`
Performs fast 90-degree orthogonal rotations without matrix multiplication:
- `cv2.ROTATE_90_CLOCKWISE`: Rotates 90 degrees clockwise.
- `cv2.ROTATE_180`: Rotates 180 degrees.
- `cv2.ROTATE_90_COUNTERCLOCKWISE`: Rotates 270 degrees clockwise (90 degrees counter-clockwise).

### 3.4 Vector Drawing Primitives: `rectangle` and `putText`
- `cv2.rectangle(img, pt1, pt2, color, thickness)`:
  - `pt1`: Top-left corner coordinate tuple `(x1, y1)`.
  - `pt2`: Bottom-right corner coordinate tuple `(x2, y2)`.
  - `color`: BGR color tuple (e.g., pure blue: `(255, 0, 0)`).
  - `thickness`: Line thickness in pixels. Passing `-1` fills the rectangle completely.
- `cv2.putText(img, text, org, fontFace, fontScale, color, thickness)`:
  - `org`: Bottom-left text baseline coordinate tuple `(x, y)`.
  - `fontFace`: Font style identifier (e.g., `cv2.FONT_HERSHEY_SIMPLEX`).

## 4. Code Implementation Examples

```python
import cv2
import numpy as np

# Load color test image
img = cv2.imread("Images/pepper.bmp")
if img is None:
    raise FileNotFoundError("Image file 'Images/pepper.bmp' not found.")

h, w = img.shape[:2]

# 1. Rescaling by a factor of 0.5 (dividing dimensions by 2)
new_w = w // 2
new_h = h // 2
half_scaled = cv2.resize(img, (new_w, new_h), interpolation=cv2.INTER_LINEAR)

# 2. Extract bounding box Region of Interest (ROI)
# Defined boundary: y from 30 to 150, x from 200 to 400
y_start, y_end = 30, 150
x_start, x_end = 200, 400

# Perform isolated extraction using .copy()
cropped_roi = img[y_start:y_end, x_start:x_end].copy()

# 3. Geometric Annotation: Draw bounding box and text label
annotated_canvas = img.copy()
# Draw bounding box outline (Color: Blue (255, 0, 0), Thickness: 2px)
cv2.rectangle(annotated_canvas, (x_start, y_start), (x_end, y_end), (255, 0, 0), thickness=2)

# Render text string above bounding box (Color: Green (0, 255, 0), Scale: 0.6)
cv2.putText(
    annotated_canvas, 
    "My rectangle", 
    (x_start, y_start - 10), 
    cv2.FONT_HERSHEY_SIMPLEX, 
    fontScale=0.6, 
    color=(0, 255, 0), 
    thickness=2
)

# 4. Orthogonal rotation (90 degrees clockwise)
rotated_90 = cv2.rotate(img, cv2.ROTATE_90_CLOCKWISE)

# Export processed outputs
cv2.imwrite("Images/pepper_half_scaled.bmp", half_scaled)
cv2.imwrite("Images/pepper_cropped_roi.bmp", cropped_roi)
cv2.imwrite("Images/pepper_annotated.bmp", annotated_canvas)
cv2.imwrite("Images/pepper_rotated_90.bmp", rotated_90)
```

# 18. Visual Frequency and Radiometry - Histograms, Equalization, AHE, and CLAHE

## 1. Overview
Radiometric operations adjust an image's tonal distribution across its dynamic range. This note covers continuous and discrete intensity histograms, dynamic range expansion (contrast stretching), global histogram equalization, and Contrast Limited Adaptive Histogram Equalization (CLAHE).

```mermaid
mindmap
  root((Radiometric Enhancement))
    Visual Frequency
      Probability Mass Function PMF
      Cumulative Distribution Function CDF
      Color Channel Histograms
    Contrast Stretching
      Dynamic Range Expansion
      Linear Min-Max Normalization
      Noise Threshold Clipping
    Equalization Systems
      Global Transform equalizeHist
      Adaptive Equalization AHE
      Contrast Limited CLAHE
```

## 2. Theoretical Formulations

### 2.1 The Discrete Image Histogram
For a single-channel image $I$ with $K$ discrete intensity levels (typically $K = 256$ for 8-bit unsigned images, spanning $[0, 255]$), the raw histogram $H(k)$ represents the total count of pixels having intensity $k$:

$$H(k) = \sum_{y=0}^{H-1} \sum_{x=0}^{W-1} \delta(I(y, x) - k), \quad k \in [0, K-1]$$

Where $\delta(\cdot)$ is the discrete Kronecker delta function. Normalizing $H(k)$ by the total number of pixels $N = H \times W$ yields the empirical **Probability Mass Function (PMF)**:

$$P(k) = \frac{H(k)}{N}$$

The **Cumulative Distribution Function (CDF)** models the cumulative probability that a randomly chosen pixel has an intensity less than or equal to $k$:

$$\text{CDF}(k) = \sum_{j=0}^{k} P(j)$$

```mermaid
graph LR
    subgraph Raw Acquisition
        Underexposed[Underexposed: Histogram concentrated at left peak]
        Overexposed[Overexposed: Histogram concentrated at right peak]
        LowContrast[Low Contrast: Histogram concentrated in narrow central band]
    end
    subgraph Radiometric Correction
        Stretching[Dynamic Range Expansion: Stretches limits to 0 and 255]
        Equalization[Histogram Equalization: Linearizes CDF to uniform distribution]
    end
    LowContrast --> Stretching
    LowContrast --> Equalization
```

### 2.2 Dynamic Range Expansion (Contrast Stretching)
When an image uses only a narrow band of available intensities $[X_{\min}, X_{\max}]$, contrast stretching remaps values across the full dynamic range $[Y_{\min}, Y_{\max}]$ (typically $[0, 255]$):

$$Y(y, x) = \alpha + \beta \cdot X(y, x)$$

Where the linear parameters $\alpha$ and $\beta$ are defined by:

$$\beta = \frac{Y_{\max} - Y_{\min}}{X_{\max} - X_{\min}}, \quad \alpha = \frac{Y_{\min} X_{\max} - Y_{\max} X_{\min}}{X_{\max} - X_{\min}}$$

Substituting $Y_{\min} = 0$ and $Y_{\max} = 255$ yields the standard contrast stretching formula:

$$Y(y, x) = \text{round}\left( \frac{255}{X_{\max} - X_{\min}} \cdot (X(y, x) - X_{\min}) \right)$$

To avoid skewing the range with isolated dead or hot pixels, $X_{\min}$ and $X_{\max}$ are determined using noise thresholds (percentile cutoffs).

### 2.3 Global Histogram Equalization
Global equalization applies a monotonic transformation function $T(k)$ designed to yield an output image with a uniform histogram distribution. This transformation uses the scaled Cumulative Distribution Function:

$$s_k = T(k) = \text{round}\left( (K - 1) \cdot \text{CDF}(k) \right) = \text{round}\left( \frac{\text{Max}}{N} \cdot \sum_{j=0}^{k} H(j) \right)$$

Where $\text{Max} = 255$ for 8-bit images.

### 2.4 Contrast Limited Adaptive Histogram Equalization (CLAHE)
Global equalization often over-amplifies background noise in homogeneous regions. CLAHE addresses this by:
1. Partitioning the image into contextual rectangular tiles (e.g., $8 \times 8$ grids).
2. Computing local histograms and clipping them at a specific threshold (`clipLimit`). Clipped pixels are redistributed uniformly across all bins before calculating the CDF.
3. Combining neighboring tile boundaries using bilinear interpolation to remove artificial block borders.

## 3. Function Reference

### 3.1 `cv2.calcHist(images, channels, mask, histSize, ranges)`
Computes intensity histograms across arbitrary channel arrays.
- `images`: Input tensor list `[img]`.
- `channels`: List of channel indices to compute (e.g., `[0]` for grayscale, `[0]`, `[1]`, `[2]` for BGR).
- `mask`: Optional binary array to limit calculation to specific regions. Use `None` for the full image.
- `histSize`: Bin counts specified as a list (e.g., `[256]`).
- `ranges`: Intensity boundaries, specified as `[0, 256]` (upper limit is exclusive).

### 3.2 `cv2.normalize(src, dst, alpha, beta, norm_type)`
Normalizes matrix values across a designated range:
- `norm_type = cv2.NORM_MINMAX`: Maps minimum values to `alpha` ($0$) and maximum values to `beta` ($255$).

### 3.3 `cv2.equalizeHist(src)`
Performs global histogram equalization on an 8-bit single-channel image.

### 3.4 `cv2.createCLAHE(clipLimit, tileGridSize)`
Initializes a CLAHE object:
- `clipLimit`: Threshold for histogram clipping (default: $40.0$, typical values: $2.0\text{--}4.0$).
- `tileGridSize`: Dimensions of contextual grid cells (e.g., `(8, 8)`).

## 4. Code Implementation Examples

```python
import cv2
import numpy as np
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt

# 1. Manual Histogram vs OpenCV Verification
def compute_histogram_pure_python(gray_matrix: np.ndarray) -> np.ndarray:
    """Computes a 256-bin intensity histogram using low-level array scanning."""
    histogram = np.zeros(256, dtype=np.int64)
    flat_pixels = gray_matrix.ravel()
    for pixel in flat_pixels:
        histogram[pixel] += 1
    return histogram

gray_img = cv2.imread("Images/cameraman.bmp", cv2.IMREAD_GRAYSCALE)
if gray_img is None:
    raise FileNotFoundError("File 'Images/cameraman.bmp' not found.")

# Compute histogram with OpenCV
cv_hist = cv2.calcHist([gray_img], [0], None, [256], [0, 256]).ravel()
py_hist = compute_histogram_pure_python(gray_img)

# Verify that both methods yield identical counts
assert np.array_equal(cv_hist.astype(np.int64), py_hist), "Histograms do not match."

# 2. Custom Dynamic Range Expansion (Contrast Stretching)
def expand_dynamic_range(image: np.ndarray, threshold: int = 0, y_min: int = 0, y_max: int = 255) -> np.ndarray:
    """Expands dynamic range using noise-tolerant min/max boundaries."""
    hist = np.bincount(image.ravel(), minlength=256)
    
    # Locate lowest and highest bins that exceed the noise threshold
    valid_bins = np.where(hist > threshold)[0]
    x_min = valid_bins[0]
    x_max = valid_bins[-1]
    
    if x_max == x_min:
        return image.copy()
        
    # Apply contrast stretching formula
    stretched = ((image.astype(np.float64) - x_min) / (x_max - x_min)) * (y_max - y_min) + y_min
    return np.clip(np.round(stretched), y_min, y_max).astype(np.uint8)

stretched_image = expand_dynamic_range(gray_img, threshold=5, y_min=0, y_max=255)

# 3. CLAHE Local Equalization on Grayscale and Color Images
clahe_operator = cv2.createCLAHE(clipLimit=2.0, tileGridSize=(8, 8))
clahe_grayscale = clahe_operator.apply(gray_img)

# For color images: Equalize the Luminance channel in LAB space to preserve colors
color_img = cv2.imread("Images/pepper.bmp")
lab_canvas = cv2.cvtColor(color_img, cv2.COLOR_BGR2LAB)
l_chan, a_chan, b_chan = cv2.split(lab_canvas)

# Equalize only the lightness (L) channel
l_clahe = clahe_operator.apply(l_chan)
merged_lab = cv2.merge([l_clahe, a_chan, b_chan])
clahe_color = cv2.cvtColor(merged_lab, cv2.COLOR_LAB2BGR)
```

# 19. Spatial Image Restoration - Noise Models, Linear Convolution, and Fast Integral Filters

## 1. Overview
Digital images can suffer from various forms of corruption during acquisition, transmission, or sensor readout. This note covers mathematical noise models, 2D discrete convolution, box and Gaussian filtering, and $O(1)$ fast box filtering using integral images (summed-area tables).

```mermaid
mindmap
  root((Restoration & Smoothing))
    Corruption Models
      Additive Gaussian AWGN
      Impulse Salt and Pepper
      Multiplicative Speckle
    Linear Convolution
      2D Discrete Sliding Kernels
      Box Filter Spatial Averaging
      Isotropic Gaussian Bell Function
    Computational Speedups
      Separable Filter Factorization
      Integral Image Summed-Area Table
      Constant Time O 1 Complexity
```

## 2. Mathematical Noise Models

```mermaid
graph LR
    subgraph Additive White Gaussian Noise
        AWGN[Continuous Gaussian Distribution: Bell curve perturbs every pixel]
    end
    subgraph Impulse Noise
        SPN[Salt & Pepper Noise: Stochastic probability sets random pixels to 0 or 255]
    end
```

### 2.1 Additive White Gaussian Noise (AWGN)
Gaussian noise models thermal and electronic sensor fluctuations. It perturbs every pixel independently according to a normal distribution:

$$I_{\text{noisy}}(y, x) = \text{clip}\Big(I(y, x) + \eta(y, x), \ 0, \ 255\Big), \quad \eta(y, x) \sim \mathcal{N}(\mu, \sigma^2)$$

Where $\mu = 0$ is the mean error and $\sigma^2$ is the noise variance. As variance $\sigma^2$ increases, high-frequency image details are progressively obscured.

### 2.2 Impulse Noise (Salt-and-Pepper)
Impulse noise models transmission errors or faulty sensor photodiode elements. It alters a random fraction $p \in [0, 1]$ of pixels, replacing their values with extreme white (salt) or black (pepper) values:

$$I_{\text{noisy}}(y, x) = \begin{cases} 
255 & \text{with probability } \frac{p}{2} \quad (\text{Salt}) \\ 
0 & \text{with probability } \frac{p}{2} \quad (\text{Pepper}) \\ 
I(y, x) & \text{with probability } 1 - p \quad (\text{Uncorrupted}) 
\end{cases}$$

## 3. Linear Spatial Filtering and Convolution

Filtering modifies a pixel's value using a weighted combination of its spatial neighbors. For an odd kernel size $M \times M$ with half-width $k = \lfloor M / 2 \rfloor$:

$$I_{\text{conv}}(y, x) = \sum_{i=-k}^{k} \sum_{j=-k}^{k} I(y - i, \ x - j) \cdot K(i, j)$$

### 3.1 The Mean (Box) Filter
Replaces each pixel with the arithmetic mean of its local neighborhood:

$$K_{\text{box}} = \frac{1}{M^2} \begin{bmatrix} 1 & 1 & \cdots & 1 \\ 1 & 1 & \cdots & 1 \\ \vdots & \vdots & \ddots & \vdots \\ 1 & 1 & \cdots & 1 \end{bmatrix}_{M \times M}$$

- **Characteristics**: Smooths additive Gaussian noise effectively, but blurs sharp edge boundaries and introduces subtle rectangular artifacts.

### 3.2 The Gaussian Filter
Weights neighboring pixels according to a 2D Gaussian distribution:

$$G(x, y) = \frac{1}{2\pi\sigma^2} \exp\left( -\frac{x^2 + y^2}{2\sigma^2} \right)$$

Weights decay smoothly with spatial distance, reducing high-frequency noise while better preserving broader structural boundaries.

## 4. Accelerated Box Filtering via Integral Images

Applying a large $M \times M$ filter directly requires $O(M^2)$ operations per pixel. Using an **Integral Image** (Summed-Area Table) allows computing arbitrary rectangular neighborhood sums in **$O(1)$ constant time**, regardless of kernel size.

```mermaid
graph TD
    A[Original Image I] -->|Cumulative Summation| B[Integral Image II]
    B --> C[Evaluate 4 Corner Lookups]
    C --> D[Sum Region = D + A - B - C]
    D --> E[Divide by Total Pixels M^2 in O 1 time]
```

### 4.1 Definition
The integral image $II(y, x)$ stores the sum of all pixel values located above and to the left of coordinate $(y, x)$:

$$II(y, x) = \sum_{y' \le y} \sum_{x' \le x} I(y', x')$$

The integral image can be constructed efficiently in a single pass ($O(H \cdot W)$) using dynamic programming:

$$II(y, x) = I(y, x) + II(y - 1, x) + II(y, x - 1) - II(y - 1, x - 1)$$

### 4.2 Constant Time $O(1)$ Window Summation
To calculate the sum of pixel values within a rectangular region defined by top-left corner $(y_1, x_1)$ and bottom-right corner $(y_2, x_2)$:

$$\text{Sum} = II(y_2, x_2) + II(y_1 - 1, x_1 - 1) - II(y_1 - 1, x_2) - II(y_2, x_1 - 1)$$

Dividing this sum by the window area $M^2$ yields the exact box filter output in $O(1)$ time.

## 5. Code Implementation Examples

```python
import cv2
import numpy as np

# 1. Noise Injection Functions
def add_gaussian_noise(image: np.ndarray, mean: float = 0.0, variance: float = 100.0) -> np.ndarray:
    """Injects Additive White Gaussian Noise into an image matrix."""
    sigma = np.sqrt(variance)
    gauss_noise = np.random.normal(mean, sigma, image.shape)
    noisy = image.astype(np.float64) + gauss_noise
    return np.clip(noisy, 0, 255).astype(np.uint8)

def add_salt_and_pepper_noise(image: np.ndarray, probability: float = 0.10) -> np.ndarray:
    """Injects impulse salt-and-pepper noise into an image matrix."""
    noisy = image.copy()
    random_matrix = np.random.rand(*image.shape[:2])
    
    # Salt noise (white pixels = 255)
    noisy[random_matrix < (probability / 2.0)] = 255
    # Pepper noise (black pixels = 0)
    noisy[(random_matrix >= (probability / 2.0)) & (random_matrix < probability)] = 0
    return noisy

# 2. Pure Python vs Accelerated Integral Image Box Filtering
def box_filter_integral(image: np.ndarray, ksize: int) -> np.ndarray:
    """Fast box filtering in O(1) time using an integral image."""
    h, w = image.shape[:2]
    pad = ksize // 2
    padded = np.pad(image.astype(np.float64), pad, mode="reflect")
    
    # Compute Integral Image
    integral = np.zeros((padded.shape[0] + 1, padded.shape[1] + 1), dtype=np.float64)
    integral[1:, 1:] = np.cumsum(np.cumsum(padded, axis=0), axis=1)
    
    filtered = np.zeros((h, w), dtype=np.uint8)
    area = ksize * ksize
    
    for y in range(h):
        for x in range(w):
            y1, x1 = y, x
            y2, x2 = y + ksize, x + ksize
            window_sum = integral[y2, x2] + integral[y1, x1] - integral[y1, x2] - integral[y2, x1]
            filtered[y, x] = int(round(window_sum / area))
            
    return filtered
```

## Integrated Subject Knowledge: 01.13. SciPy Signal and Spatial Operations - 2D Filtering and Morphology

The `scipy.ndimage` and `scipy.signal` modules provide scientific algorithms for discrete 2D spatial filtering, frequency responses, and multi-dimensional analysis.

```mermaid
mindmap
  root((SciPy Spatial Analysis))
    Discrete Filtering
      scipy.signal.convolve2d
      Mode Arguments full same valid
      Boundary Conditions fill reflect
    Morphology Engine
      scipy.ndimage.binary_erosion
      scipy.ndimage.binary_dilation
      Arbitrary Structuring Elements
```

### 1. 2D Spatial Convolution: `scipy.signal.convolve2d(in1, in2, mode='full', boundary='fill', fillvalue=0)`
Computes the mathematically exact discrete two-dimensional convolution of two matrices:

* `mode`:
  * `'full'`: Computes convolution across all points of overlap. Output shape: $(M_1 + M_2 - 1, N_1 + N_2 - 1)$.
  * `'same'`: Returns the central portion of the convolution, matching the shape of `in1`.
  * `'valid'`: Computes output only for positions where the filter kernel overlaps completely with the input image. Output shape: $(M_1 - M_2 + 1, N_1 - N_2 + 1)$.
* `boundary`: Method for handling image edges:
  * `'fill'`: Pads borders with a constant value specified by `fillvalue`.
  * `'symm'` / `'reflect'`: Mirrors pixel values across the boundary.
  * `'wrap'`: Treats the image as periodic, wrapping values from the opposite edge.

```python
import numpy as np
from scipy import signal

# Define a 5x5 test image and a 3x3 averaging kernel
image = np.ones((5, 5), dtype=np.float32)
kernel = np.full((3, 3), 1.0 / 9.0, dtype=np.float32)

# Compute convolution with reflection padding, maintaining identical output dimensions
smooth = signal.convolve2d(image, kernel, mode='same', boundary='symm')
```

### 01.14. Scikit-Learn Primitives - Clustering, Dimensionality, and Metrics.md

# Scikit-Learn Primitives: Clustering, Dimensionality, and Metrics

Scikit-Learn (`sklearn`) provides machine learning algorithms used in mid-level image processing, such as color-based pixel clustering, dimensionality reduction, and evaluation metrics.

```mermaid
mindmap
  root((Scikit-Learn Primitives))
    Unsupervised Clustering
      KMeans Color Quantization
      DBSCAN Density Segmentation
    Evaluation Metrics
      metrics.jaccard_score IoU
      metrics.confusion_matrix
    Feature Transformation
      StandardScaler Feature Normalization
```

### 1. Pixel Feature Clustering: `sklearn.cluster.KMeans`
Partitions pixel colors into $K$ discrete clusters to perform color quantization or unsupervised segmentation:

```python
import numpy as np
from sklearn.cluster import KMeans

# Synthesize an image: 100 rows, 100 cols, 3 color channels
img = np.random.randint(0, 256, size=(100, 100, 3), dtype=np.uint8)

# Reshape the 3D image into a 2D feature matrix: (N_pixels, 3_features)
pixel_features = img.reshape((-1, 3)).astype(np.float32)

# Fit KMeans to group pixels into 3 color clusters
kmeans = KMeans(n_clusters=3, random_state=42, n_init='auto')
labels = kmeans.fit_predict(pixel_features)

# Reshape the 1D cluster labels back into the original 2D image shape
segmented_labels = labels.reshape((100, 100))
```

### 2. Segmentation Evaluation: `sklearn.metrics.jaccard_score`
Calculates the Jaccard similarity index (Intersection-over-Union, IoU) to measure overlap between a predicted binary segmentation and a ground-truth reference:

$$J(A, B) = \frac{|A \cap B|}{|A \cup B|}$$

```python
from sklearn.metrics import jaccard_score

# Flatten 2D binary masks to 1D arrays
gt_mask = np.array([0, 1, 1, 0, 1, 0], dtype=np.uint8)
pred_mask = np.array([0, 1, 0, 0, 1, 0], dtype=np.uint8)

iou = jaccard_score(gt_mask, pred_mask)
print("Jaccard IoU Score:", iou)
```

# 20. Non-Linear and Edge-Preserving Filtering - Median, Bilateral, and Nagao Filters

## 1. Overview
Linear filtering blurs sharp boundaries because it computes an unweighted average of neighboring pixels across edges. Non-linear edge-preserving filters solve this by adapting smoothing to local edge orientations and radiometric differences. This note covers Median filtering, Bilateral filtering, and the directional Nagao filter.

```mermaid
mindmap
  root((Edge-Preserving Filtering))
    Order-Statistic
      Median Filter
      Impulse Salt & Pepper Removal
      Zero Edge Blurring
    Cross-Domain Range
      Bilateral Filter
      Spatial Geometric Gaussian
      Radiometric Photometric Gaussian
    Variance Selection
      Nagao Filter
      9 Directional Sub-regions
      Local Variance Minimization
```

## 2. The Median Filter (`cv2.medianBlur`)
The median filter is an order-statistic filter that replaces each pixel with the statistical median of its neighborhood $W$:

$$I_{\text{median}}(y, x) = \text{median}\Big\{ I(y + i, \ x + j) \mid (i, j) \in W \Big\}$$

### Why the Median Filter Outperforms Mean Filters on Impulse Noise
- **Mean Filter**: Incorporates corrupted values ($0$ or $255$) directly into the average, pulling the result away from the true intensity:
  $$\text{Mean}([120, 122, 121, 123, 255]) = 148.2 \quad (\text{Distorted})$$
- **Median Filter**: Discards extreme outliers during rank sorting, preserving the underlying value:
  $$\text{Median}(\text{sort}([120, 121, 122, 123, 255])) = 122 \quad (\text{Accurate})$$

## 3. The Bilateral Filter (`cv2.bilateralFilter`)
The bilateral filter combines spatial geometric proximity with radiometric intensity similarity:

$$I_{\text{bilateral}}(p) = \frac{1}{W_p} \sum_{q \in S} I(q) \cdot G_{\sigma_s}(\|p - q\|) \cdot G_{\sigma_r}(|I(p) - I(q)|)$$

Where:
- $p = (y, x)$ is the target center pixel coordinate.
- $q = (y', x')$ is an adjacent neighborhood pixel coordinate.
- $G_{\sigma_s}(\|p - q\|) = \exp\left(-\frac{\|p - q\|^2}{2\sigma_s^2}\right)$ is the **spatial Gaussian kernel**, which weights pixels by geometric distance.
- $G_{\sigma_r}(|I(p) - I(q)|) = \exp\left(-\frac{|I(p) - I(q)|^2}{2\sigma_r^2}\right)$ is the **radiometric Gaussian kernel**, which weights pixels by intensity similarity.
- $W_p = \sum_{q \in S} G_{\sigma_s}(\|p - q\|) \cdot G_{\sigma_r}(|I(p) - I(q)|)$ is a normalization factor.

When the filter encounters a high-contrast boundary, pixels on the opposite side of the edge have large intensity differences $|I(p) - I(q)|$. This drives their radiometric weight $G_{\sigma_r}$ toward zero, preventing smoothing across the edge.

## 4. The Nagao Filter

The Nagao filter is an edge-preserving operator that analyzes a $5 \times 5$ window around each pixel, partitioned into 9 directional sub-regions.

```mermaid
graph TD
    CenterPixel[Target Node y, x in 5x5 Window] --> Partitions[Evaluate 9 Directional Sub-regions]
    Partitions --> R0[Region 0: Central 3x3 Cross - 5 Pixels]
    Partitions --> R14[Regions 1-4: Cardinal Bars N, S, E, W - 7 Pixels each]
    Partitions --> R58[Regions 5-8: Corner Quadrants NE, NW, SE, SW - 7 Pixels each]
    R0 --> Stats[Compute Local Sample Variance for each Sub-region]
    R14 --> Stats
    R58 --> Stats
    Stats --> MinVar[Identify Sub-region with Minimum Variance]
    MinVar --> Output[Assign Center Pixel = Mean of Best Sub-region]
```

### 4.1 Sub-Region Geometry
Within a $5 \times 5$ window centered at $(0, 0)$, the 9 sub-regions are defined by the following relative coordinate offsets:
- **Region 0 (Central Cross - 5 pixels)**:
  $$\{(0,0), \ (-1,0), \ (1,0), \ (0,-1), \ (0,1)\}$$
- **Region 1 (North Cardinal Bar - 7 pixels)**:
  $$\{(0,0), \ (-1,0), \ (-2,0), \ (-1,-1), \ (-2,-1), \ (-1,1), \ (-2,1)\}$$
- **Region 2 (South Cardinal Bar - 7 pixels)**:
  $$\{(0,0), \ (1,0), \ (2,0), \ (1,-1), \ (2,-1), \ (1,1), \ (2,1)\}$$
- **Region 3 (East Cardinal Bar - 7 pixels)**:
  $$\{(0,0), \ (0,1), \ (0,2), \ (-1,1), \ (-1,2), \ (1,1), \ (1,2)\}$$
- **Region 4 (West Cardinal Bar - 7 pixels)**:
  $$\{(0,0), \ (0,-1), \ (0,-2), \ (-1,-1), \ (-1,-2), \ (1,-1), \ (1,-2)\}$$
- **Region 5 (Northeast Quadrant - 7 pixels)**:
  $$\{(0,0), \ (-1,0), \ (-1,1), \ (-1,2), \ (-2,1), \ (-2,2), \ (0,1)\}$$
- **Region 6 (Northwest Quadrant - 7 pixels)**:
  $$\{(0,0), \ (-1,0), \ (-1,-1), \ (-1,-2), \ (-2,-1), \ (-2,-2), \ (0,-1)\}$$
- **Region 7 (Southeast Quadrant - 7 pixels)**:
  $$\{(0,0), \ (1,0), \ (1,1), \ (1,2), \ (2,1), \ (2,2), \ (0,1)\}$$
- **Region 8 (Southwest Quadrant - 7 pixels)**:
  $$\{(0,0), \ (1,0), \ (1,-1), \ (1,-2), \ (2,-1), \ (2,-2), \ (0,-1)\}$$

### 4.2 Algorithm Steps
1. For each candidate sub-region $k \in \{0, \dots, 8\}$, compute the sample variance:
   $$\sigma_k^2 = \frac{1}{|R_k|} \sum_{(i, j) \in R_k} \Big( I(y + i, \ x + j) - \mu_k \Big)^2$$
2. Identify the sub-region that minimizes local variance:
   $$k^* = \arg\min_{k} \sigma_k^2$$
3. Replace the center pixel with the mean intensity of that lowest-variance sub-region:
   $$I_{\text{Nagao}}(y, x) = \text{round}(\mu_{k^*})$$

Because variance is high across an edge and low within homogeneous regions, this filter smooths noise while keeping edges sharp.

## 5. Code Implementation Examples

```python
import numpy as np

def nagao_filter(image: np.ndarray) -> np.ndarray:
    """Applies the 5x5 edge-preserving Nagao filter to a grayscale image."""
    h, w = image.shape[:2]
    # Apply reflect padding of 2 pixels along each boundary
    padded = np.pad(image.astype(np.float64), pad_width=2, mode="reflect")
    output = np.zeros((h, w), dtype=np.uint8)

    sub_regions = [
        [(0,0), (-1,0), (1,0), (0,-1), (0,1)],
        [(0,0), (-1,0), (-2,0), (-1,-1), (-2,-1), (-1,1), (-2,1)],
        [(0,0), (1,0), (2,0), (1,-1), (2,-1), (1,1), (2,1)],
        [(0,0), (0,1), (0,2), (-1,1), (-1,2), (1,1), (1,2)],
        [(0,0), (0,-1), (0,-2), (-1,-1), (-1,-2), (1,-1), (1,-2)],
        [(0,0), (-1,0), (-1,1), (-1,2), (-2,1), (-2,2), (0,1)],
        [(0,0), (-1,0), (-1,-1), (-1,-2), (-2,-1), (-2,-2), (0,-1)],
        [(0,0), (1,0), (1,1), (1,2), (2,1), (2,2), (0,1)],
        [(0,0), (1,0), (1,-1), (1,-2), (2,-1), (2,-2), (0,-1)]
    ]

    for y in range(h):
        for x in range(w):
            py, px = y + 2, x + 2
            lowest_variance = float("inf")
            chosen_mean = padded[py, px]

            for region in sub_regions:
                values = [padded[py + dy, px + dx] for dy, dx in region]
                var = np.var(values)
                if var < lowest_variance:
                    lowest_variance = var
                    chosen_mean = np.mean(values)

            output[y, x] = int(round(chosen_mean))

    return output
```

# 21. Mathematical Morphology - Set Theory, Structuring Elements, and Thinning

## 1. Overview
Mathematical morphology analyzes spatial shapes and geometric structures using set theory. This note covers structuring element probes, basic morphological operators (erosion, dilation, opening, closing), cell separation techniques, and the Zhang-Suen thinning algorithm.

```mermaid
mindmap
  root((Mathematical Morphology))
    Set Theoretical Primitives
      Minkowski Vector Addition
      Dilation Shape Expansion
      Erosion Kernel Subtraction
    Compound Filters
      Morphological Opening
      Morphological Closing
      Granulometric Separation
    Topological Reductions
      Medial Axis Transform
      Zhang-Suen Skeletonization
      Iterative Sub-Iteration Passes
```

## 2. Set Theory Foundations and Structuring Elements

Binary images are modeled as subsets of the discrete 2D space: $A \subseteq \mathbb{Z}^2$.
A **Structuring Element** (SE) $B \subset \mathbb{Z}^2$ is a predefined spatial shape (such as a cross, square, or disk) with an assigned origin:

```mermaid
graph TD
    B[Structuring Element Kernel B] --> Rect[cv2.MORPH_RECT: Full Grid Matrix]
    B --> Cross[cv2.MORPH_CROSS: Cardinal Axes Cross]
    B --> Ellipse[cv2.MORPH_ELLIPSE: Discretized Radial Disk]
```

### 2.1 Erosion ($A \ominus B$)
Shrinks foreground shapes by keeping only points where the structuring element fits entirely inside the foreground:

$$A \ominus B = \{ z \in \mathbb{Z}^2 \mid (B)_z \subseteq A \}$$

Where $(B)_z = \{ b + z \mid b \in B \}$ represents the translation of $B$ by spatial vector $z$.
- **Effect**: Eliminates isolated white noise pixels, shrinks object perimeters, and breaks thin bridges connecting adjacent structures.

### 2.2 Dilation ($A \oplus B$)
Expands foreground shapes by adding points where the translated structuring element touches the foreground:

$$A \oplus B = \{ z \in \mathbb{Z}^2 \mid (\hat{B})_z \cap A \neq \emptyset \}$$

Where $\hat{B} = \{ -b \mid b \in B \}$ is the reflection of $B$ around its origin.
- **Effect**: Fills small background holes, expands object boundaries, and bridges small structural gaps.

### 2.3 Morphological Opening ($A \circ B$)
Applies an erosion followed by a dilation using the same structuring element:

$$A \circ B = (A \ominus B) \oplus B$$

- **Effect**: Smooths object contours, breaks narrow isthmuses, and removes small foreground noise while preserving overall object geometry.

### 2.4 Morphological Closing ($A \bullet B$)
Applies a dilation followed by an erosion:

$$A \bullet B = (A \oplus B) \ominus B$$

- **Effect**: Fills narrow interior holes, closes narrow boundary channels, and bridges gaps without substantially changing object area.

## 3. The Zhang-Suen Skeletonization (Thinning) Algorithm

Thinning reduces binary foreground shapes to one-pixel-wide medial skeletons while preserving topological connectivity. The Zhang-Suen algorithm works by iteratively pruning contour pixels across two alternating sub-iterations.

```mermaid
graph TD
    In[Binary Shape Grid 0 and 1] --> Iter[Iterative Two-Pass Scanning Loop]
    Iter --> Sub1[Sub-Iteration 1: Test North/East Boundary Direction Constraints]
    Sub1 --> Flag1[Delete Flagged Pixels]
    Flag1 --> Sub2[Sub-Iteration 2: Test South/West Boundary Direction Constraints]
    Sub2 --> Flag2[Delete Flagged Pixels]
    Flag2 --> Check{Did any pixels change?}
    Check -->|Yes| Iter
    Check -->|No: Convergence Reached| Skele[Final One-Pixel-Wide Skeleton Output]
```

### Mathematical Conditions for Pixel Deletion
A boundary pixel $P_1$ with 8-neighbors ordered cyclically:

$$\begin{bmatrix} P_9 & P_2 & P_3 \\ P_8 & P_1 & P_4 \\ P_7 & P_6 & P_5 \end{bmatrix}$$

Is flagged for deletion if all four conditions are satisfied:
1. **Connectivity Condition**: $2 \le B(P_1) \le 6$, where $B(P_1) = \sum_{i=2}^9 P_i$ is the number of active non-zero neighbors.
2. **Transition Count**: $A(P_1) = 1$, where $A(P_1)$ is the number of $0 \rightarrow 1$ patterns in the circular sequence $P_2, P_3, P_4, P_5, P_6, P_7, P_8, P_9, P_2$.
3. **Directional Constraint (Sub-iteration 1)**:
   $$P_2 \cdot P_4 \cdot P_6 = 0 \quad \text{and} \quad P_4 \cdot P_6 \cdot P_8 = 0$$
4. **Directional Constraint (Sub-iteration 2)**:
   $$P_2 \cdot P_4 \cdot P_8 = 0 \quad \text{and} \quad P_2 \cdot P_6 \cdot P_8 = 0$$

The algorithm repeats until no further pixels are flagged for deletion.

## 4. Code Implementation Examples

```python
import cv2
import numpy as np

# 1. Morphological Operations with OpenCV
binary_image = cv2.imread("Images/Globules_rouges.jpg", cv2.IMREAD_GRAYSCALE)
_, binary_thresh = cv2.threshold(binary_image, 127, 255, cv2.THRESH_BINARY)

# Construct flat cross-shaped and elliptical structuring elements
se_rect_3x3 = cv2.getStructuringElement(cv2.MORPH_RECT, (3, 3))
se_ellipse_5x5 = cv2.getStructuringElement(cv2.MORPH_ELLIPSE, (5, 5))

# Apply primitives
eroded = cv2.erode(binary_thresh, se_rect_3x3, iterations=1)
dilated = cv2.dilate(binary_thresh, se_rect_3x3, iterations=1)
opened = cv2.morphologyEx(binary_thresh, cv2.MORPH_OPEN, se_ellipse_5x5)
closed = cv2.morphologyEx(binary_thresh, cv2.MORPH_CLOSE, se_ellipse_5x5)

# 2. Automated Cell Separation Pipeline (Watershed / Granulometry)
def separate_overlapping_cells(binary_mask: np.ndarray) -> np.ndarray:
    """Separates touching circular cells using distance transforms and opening."""
    # Remove boundary noise with morphological opening
    opened_mask = cv2.morphologyEx(binary_mask, cv2.MORPH_OPEN, np.ones((3, 3), np.uint8), iterations=2)
    # Compute Euclidean distance transform from background boundaries
    dist_transform = cv2.distanceTransform(opened_mask, cv2.DIST_L2, 5)
    # Threshold distance peaks to identify cell centers
    _, sure_fg = cv2.threshold(dist_transform, 0.4 * dist_transform.max(), 255, cv2.THRESH_BINARY)
    return sure_fg.astype(np.uint8)

# 3. Zhang-Suen Skeletonization
# Available via OpenCV Contrib: cv2.ximgproc.thinning
try:
    skeleton = cv2.ximgproc.thinning(binary_thresh, thinningType=cv2.ximgproc.THINNING_ZHANGSUEN)
except AttributeError:
    print("opencv-contrib-python required for cv2.ximgproc.thinning.")
```

# 22. Objective Image Quality Assessment - MSE, PSNR, and SSIM Metrics

## 1. Overview
Objective image quality metrics provide quantitative benchmarks to assess fidelity after restoration, filtering, or super-resolution. This note covers Mean Squared Error (MSE), Peak Signal-to-Noise Ratio (PSNR), and the Structural Similarity Index (SSIM).

```mermaid
mindmap
  root((Quality Assessment))
    Error Energy
      Mean Squared Error MSE
      Root Mean Squared Error RMSE
      Mathematical Power Metrics
    Logarithmic Ratio
      Peak Signal to Noise Ratio PSNR
      Decibel Log10 Scaling
      Dynamic Range Representation
    Perceptual Systems
      Structural Similarity SSIM
      Luminance Correlation
      Contrast Comparison
```

## 2. Mathematical Formulations

```mermaid
graph LR
    subgraph Signal Processing Error Metric
        MSE[MSE: Sum of Squared Differences] --> PSNR[PSNR: Logarithmic Ratio relative to Peak Signal]
    end
    subgraph Human Visual System HVS
        SSIM[SSIM: Joint Assessment of Luminance, Contrast, and Structure]
    end
```

### 2.1 Mean Squared Error (MSE)
Calculates the average squared difference between an original reference image $I$ and a modified or degraded image $\hat{I}$, across dimensions $H \times W$:

$$\text{MSE} = \frac{1}{H \cdot W} \sum_{y=0}^{H-1} \sum_{x=0}^{W-1} \Big( I(y, x) - \hat{I}(y, x) \Big)^2$$

- **Ideal Value**: $0$ indicates identical images.
- **Limitation**: Sensitive to global brightness offsets that may not significantly impact perceived visual quality.

### 2.2 Peak Signal-to-Noise Ratio (PSNR)
Expresses reconstruction fidelity on a logarithmic decibel (dB) scale, comparing maximum possible signal power to the error power:

$$\text{PSNR} = 10 \cdot \log_{10} \left( \frac{R^2}{\text{MSE}} \right)$$

Where $R$ is the dynamic range of the image data type:
- For 8-bit unsigned integers (`uint8`), $R = 2^{8} - 1 = 255$.
- For normalized floating-point images (`float64`), $R = 1.0$.

$$\text{PSNR} = 10 \cdot \log_{10} \left( \frac{255^2}{\text{MSE}} \right) = 20 \cdot \log_{10}(255) - 10 \cdot \log_{10}(\text{MSE}) \approx 48.13 - 10 \cdot \log_{10}(\text{MSE})$$

- If $\text{MSE} \to 0$, $\text{PSNR} \to \infty$ (identical signals).
- Typical reconstruction values: $30\text{--}45\text{ dB}$ for high-quality restoration; $<20\text{ dB}$ for severely corrupted rasters.

### 2.3 Structural Similarity Index (SSIM)
While PSNR measures raw numerical error, SSIM evaluates image degradation based on three perceived visual components: **luminance** ($l$), **contrast** ($c$), and **structural correlation** ($s$):

$$\text{SSIM}(x, y) = \Big[ l(x, y) \Big]^\alpha \cdot \Big[ c(x, y) \Big]^\beta \cdot \Big[ s(x, y) \Big]^\gamma$$

Setting exponents $\alpha = \beta = \gamma = 1$ yields the standard form:

$$\text{SSIM}(x, y) = \frac{(2\mu_x\mu_y + C_1)(2\sigma_{xy} + C_2)}{(\mu_x^2 + \mu_y^2 + C_1)(\sigma_x^2 + \sigma_y^2 + C_2)}$$

Where:
- $\mu_x, \mu_y$: Local mean intensities.
- $\sigma_x^2, \sigma_y^2$: Local sample variances.
- $\sigma_{xy}$: Cross-covariance between signals $x$ and $y$.
- $C_1 = (K_1 R)^2, \ C_2 = (K_2 R)^2$: Regularization constants (typically $K_1 = 0.01, \ K_2 = 0.03$) preventing division by zero when denominators are near zero.

## 3. Python Implementations

```python
import numpy as np

def calculate_mse(original: np.ndarray, distorted: np.ndarray) -> float:
    """Computes Mean Squared Error between two matrices."""
    if original.shape != distorted.shape:
        raise ValueError("Image matrices must have identical dimensions.")
    diff = original.astype(np.float64) - distorted.astype(np.float64)
    return float(np.mean(diff ** 2))

def calculate_psnr(original: np.ndarray, distorted: np.ndarray, max_val: float = 255.0) -> float:
    """Computes Peak Signal-to-Noise Ratio in decibels (dB)."""
    mse = calculate_mse(original, distorted)
    if mse == 0.0:
        return float("inf")  # Perfect reconstruction
    return float(10.0 * np.log10((max_val ** 2) / mse))

def calculate_ssim_windowed(img1: np.ndarray, img2: np.ndarray) -> float:
    """Computes global Mean Structural Similarity Index across matrices."""
    c1 = (0.01 * 255) ** 2
    c2 = (0.03 * 255) ** 2
    
    img1 = img1.astype(np.float64)
    img2 = img2.astype(np.float64)
    
    mu1 = np.mean(img1)
    mu2 = np.mean(img2)
    
    sigma1_sq = np.var(img1)
    sigma2_sq = np.var(img2)
    sigma12 = np.mean((img1 - mu1) * (img2 - mu2))
    
    numerator = (2.0 * mu1 * mu2 + c1) * (2.0 * sigma12 + c2)
    denominator = (mu1**2 + mu2**2 + c1) * (sigma1_sq + sigma2_sq + c2)
    
    return float(numerator / denominator)
```

# 23. Super-Resolution and Edge-Adaptive Interpolation - Pascal Getreuer Contour Stencils

## 1. Overview and Problem Formulation
Single-frame image super-resolution reconstructs a high-resolution (HR) image from a low-resolution (LR) input. While standard linear interpolation methods (bilinear, bicubic) produce blurry or jagged edges, Pascal Getreuer's (2009) **Contour Stencils** algorithm preserves sharpness by estimating local edge orientations and interpolating **along** contours rather than across them.

```mermaid
mindmap
  root((Contour Stencils Super-Resolution))
    Core Mathematical Theory
      Total Variation TV Along Curves
      Minimizing TV Over Stencil Sets
      Bounding Interpolation Error
    Directional Modeling
      12 Geometric Stencil Sets
      8 Discretized Contour Orientations
      Sub-Pixel Grid Coordinate Allocation
    Edge Reconstruction
      Diagonal Sub-Pixel Central Point X
      Boundary Midpoint Averaging M
      Iterative Resolution Stacking 2x to 4x
```

## 2. Mathematical Foundations: Total Variation Along Curves
Reference: Pascal Getreuer, *Contour stencils for edge-adaptive image interpolation*, SPIE Proceedings Vol. 7257, 2009.

Interpolating across an edge creates blurring, while interpolating **along** an edge preserves sharpness. For a smooth candidate curve $C$ parameterized by $\gamma(t)$ for $t \in [0, T]$, its Total Variation (TV) is:

$$\text{TV}(C) = \int_{0}^{T} \left| \frac{\partial}{\partial t} u(\gamma(t)) \right| dt$$

If $\text{TV}(C) \approx 0$, pixel intensity is nearly constant along the curve, meaning it closely follows an image contour.

### 2.1 Error Bound Theorem (Getreuer Theorem 3.1)
Consider approximating intensity $u(x)$ along a curve segment passing through nodes $a, x, b$ with linear weights:
$$\hat{u}(x) = (1 - \lambda) u(a) + \lambda u(b), \quad 0 \le \lambda \le 1$$
The approximation error is bounded by the curve's Total Variation:

$$|\hat{u}(x) - u(x)| \le \max\Big( |1 - \lambda|, \ |\lambda| \Big) \cdot \text{TV}(C)$$

**Core Insight**: Selecting candidate curves that minimize $\text{TV}(C)$ directly minimizes the upper bound on interpolation error.

```mermaid
graph TD
    Cell[Sub-Pixel Cell i, j] --> Eval[Evaluate 12 Candidate Stencils TV_i,j S]
    Eval --> ArgMin[Find Stencil S* Minimizing TV]
    ArgMin --> DirectInterp[Interpolate Cell Center X Along Contour Direction]
    DirectInterp --> Midpoints[Average Shared Edge Midpoints with Adjacent Cells]
```

## 3. Discrete Contour Stencils and Sub-Pixel Formulas

In a factor-2 scaling setup, each $2 \times 2$ pixel cell on the low-resolution grid contains four known corner samples:
- Top-left: $v_{i, j}$
- Top-right: $v_{i, j+1}$
- Bottom-left: $v_{i+1, j}$
- Bottom-right: $v_{i+1, j+1}$

The goal is to compute the missing sub-pixel points:
- Cell center: $X$
- Edge midpoints: $N, S, W, E$

```text
Known LR:    v(i,j) -------- N -------- v(i, j+1)
               |                         |
               W            X            E
               |                         |
Known LR:    v(i+1,j) ------ S -------- v(i+1, j+1)
```

### 3.1 Center Point Interpolation ($X$)
Evaluating Total Variation across the cell's two diagonal directions:
- Diagonal 1: $\text{TV}_{\text{diag1}} = |v_{i, j} - v_{i+1, j+1}|$
- Diagonal 2: $\text{TV}_{\text{diag2}} = |v_{i+1, j} - v_{i, j+1}|$

The center point $X$ is interpolated along the lower-variation direction:

$$X = \begin{cases} 
\frac{1}{2} (v_{i, j} + v_{i+1, j+1}) & \text{if } \text{TV}_{\text{diag1}} < \text{TV}_{\text{diag2}} \\ 
\frac{1}{2} (v_{i+1, j} + v_{i, j+1}) & \text{if } \text{TV}_{\text{diag2}} < \text{TV}_{\text{diag1}} \\ 
\frac{1}{4} (v_{i, j} + v_{i+1, j} + v_{i, j+1} + v_{i+1, j+1}) & \text{if } \text{TV}_{\text{diag1}} = \text{TV}_{\text{diag2}} 
\end{cases}$$

### 3.2 Shared Edge Midpoint Averaging
Edge midpoints ($N, S, W, E$) are shared between adjacent cells. For instance, the boundary midpoint $M$ between horizontal cells $(i, j)$ and $(i+1, j)$ is computed as the average of the two independently assigned values:

$$M = \frac{1}{2} \left( E_{(i, j)} + W'_{(i+1, j)} \right)$$

## 4. Complete Code Implementation

```python
import numpy as np

def contour_stencil_super_resolution_2x(lr_image: np.ndarray) -> np.ndarray:
    """
    Performs 2x single-frame super-resolution using Pascal Getreuer's Contour Stencils.
    Preserves edge sharpness by interpolating along paths of minimum Total Variation.
    """
    h, w = lr_image.shape[:2]
    out_h = 2 * h - 1
    out_w = 2 * w - 1
    hr_output = np.zeros((out_h, out_w), dtype=np.float64)

    # Step 1: Copy known low-resolution samples to even grid coordinates
    for y in range(h):
        for x in range(w):
            hr_output[2 * y, 2 * x] = lr_image[y, x]

    # Step 2: Compute cell centers (X) along paths of minimum Total Variation
    for y in range(h - 1):
        for x in range(w - 1):
            v00 = float(lr_image[y, x])
            v01 = float(lr_image[y, x + 1])
            v10 = float(lr_image[y + 1, x])
            v11 = float(lr_image[y + 1, x + 1])

            tv_diag1 = abs(v00 - v11)
            tv_diag2 = abs(v10 - v01)

            if tv_diag1 < tv_diag2:
                center_val = 0.5 * (v00 + v11)
            elif tv_diag2 < tv_diag1:
                center_val = 0.5 * (v10 + v01)
            else:
                center_val = 0.25 * (v00 + v01 + v10 + v11)

            hr_output[2 * y + 1, 2 * x + 1] = center_val

    # Step 3: Interpolate intermediate edge midpoints
    for y in range(out_h):
        for x in range(out_w):
            if y % 2 == 1 and x % 2 == 0:
                # Vertical edge midpoint
                neighbors = []
                if y > 0:
                    neighbors.append(hr_output[y - 1, x])
                if y < out_h - 1:
                    neighbors.append(hr_output[y + 1, x])
                hr_output[y, x] = np.mean(neighbors)

            elif y % 2 == 0 and x % 2 == 1:
                # Horizontal edge midpoint
                neighbors = []
                if x > 0:
                    neighbors.append(hr_output[y, x - 1])
                if x < out_w - 1:
                    neighbors.append(hr_output[y, x + 1])
                hr_output[y, x] = np.mean(neighbors)

    return np.clip(np.round(hr_output), 0, 255).astype(np.uint8)
```

            "00. Master Curriculum Roadmap.md"
        ),

    ROGUE_FOLDERS = [
        "00. Navigation",
        "02. Foundations",
        "03. Preprocessing",
        "04. Segmentation",
        "05. Super Resolution",
        "06. Laboratories",
        "07. Exercises",
        "09. Projects",
    ]

    def __init__(self, vault_dir: Path, dry_run: bool = False):
        self.vault_dir = vault_dir.resolve()
        self.dry_run = dry_run
        self.link_map: dict[str, str] = {}
        for src, dest in self.CONSOLIDATION_PLAN.items():
            self.link_map[Path(src).stem] = Path(dest).stem

    def create_backup(self) -> Path:
        timestamp = datetime.now().strftime("%Y%m%d_%H%M%S")
        backup_zip = self.vault_dir.parent / f"backup_before_unify_{timestamp}.zip"
        print(f"[*] Creating zip backup of vault at: {backup_zip.name}")
        with zipfile.ZipFile(backup_zip, "w", zipfile.ZIP_DEFLATED) as zf:
            for root, _, files in os.walk(self.vault_dir):
                for f in files:
                    full_p = Path(root) / f
                    zf.write(full_p, full_p.relative_to(self.vault_dir))
        print(f"[OK] Backup created.\n")
        return backup_zip

    @staticmethod
    def extract_unique_body(src_text: str) -> str:
        """Strips duplicate top-level title and clean lines from source note."""
        lines = src_text.splitlines()
        # Skip leading title if present
        while lines and (lines[0].startswith("# ") or not lines[0].strip() or lines[0].strip() == "---"):
            lines.pop(0)
        return "\n".join(lines).strip()

    def merge_and_relocate(self):
        print("[*] Merging fragmented content into authoritative chapter files...")
        merged_count = 0

        for src_rel, dest_rel in self.CONSOLIDATION_PLAN.items():
            src_file = self.vault_dir / src_rel
            dest_file = self.vault_dir / dest_rel

            if not src_file.exists():
                continue

            src_content = src_file.read_text(encoding="utf-8")
            clean_body = self.extract_unique_body(src_content)

            if not dest_file.exists():
                # Destination does not exist: create it with the source content
                if not self.dry_run:
                    dest_file.parent.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)
                    dest_file.write_text(src_content, encoding="utf-8")
                    src_file.unlink()
                print(f"  [Created Target] {dest_rel}")
            else:
                # Destination exists: append source content under an integrated section
                dest_content = dest_file.read_text(encoding="utf-8")
                # Avoid exact duplicate content injection
                if clean_body[:100].lower() not in dest_content.lower():
                    heading = f"\n\n---\n\n## Integrated Subject Knowledge: {src_file.stem}\n\n"
                    merged = dest_content.rstrip() + heading + clean_body + "\n"
                    if not self.dry_run:
                        dest_file.write_text(merged, encoding="utf-8")
                        src_file.unlink()
                    print(f"  [Merged Content] {src_rel} -> {dest_rel}")
                else:
                    if not self.dry_run:
                        src_file.unlink()
                    print(f"  [Already Merged / Removed] {src_rel}")

            merged_count += 1

        print(f"[OK] Consolidated {merged_count} notes.\n")

    def remove_rogue_folders(self):
        print("[*] Deleting rogue duplicate folders from top level...")
        for folder_name in self.ROGUE_FOLDERS:
            folder_path = self.vault_dir / folder_name
            if folder_path.exists() and folder_path.is_dir():
                if not self.dry_run:
                    shutil.rmtree(folder_path, ignore_errors=True)
                print(f"  [DELETED FOLDER] {folder_name}/")

        print("[OK] Top-level vault cleaned of all duplicate green folders.\n")

    def update_wikilinks(self):
        print("[*] Harmonizing Obsidian wikilinks across all notes...")
        updated = 0
        link_regex = re.compile(r"\[\[([^\|\]]+?)(\|[^\]]+)?\]\]")

        def replace_target(m: re.Match) -> str:
            target = m.group(1).strip()
            display = m.group(2) or ""
            if target in self.link_map:
                new_stem = self.link_map[target]
                return f"[[{new_stem}{display}]]"
            r

========================================================================
